MULTIVERSE

MULTIVERSE is a philosophical model by karbytes on the karlina_object site. It begins with nature as the complete, immutable collection of universes. It then moves inside one universe to ask how states succeed one another, and inside one observer to ask how a physical system renders a world to itself. A single page therefore holds three different scales of description. Nature contains histories. A universe has a history. An agent inhabits part of that history through a model it continually renders. The closing argument returns to the immediate event of reading. Whatever else is true of the proposed multiverse, the appearance of a sentence gives the reader a concrete instance of something occurring.

The page and its illustration

The masthead carries the site name karlina_object and the tagline “Creativity And The Cosmos.” MULTIVERSE appears as the page title and again above the illustration. A line below the figure identifies a separately stored copy of the image. The yellow-highlighted opening states the relationship between nature and the multiverse. The three main sections define UNIVERSE, SUBJECTIVITY, and MULTIVERSE, with horizontal rules marking the transitions. Three preformatted boxes set out the two models of endless time and the model of nested simulation. An italic instruction tells the reader to scroll those boxes horizontally when necessary. Near the end, cyan highlighting draws attention to the argument from the sentence currently being rendered. The page closes with 09_JULY_2025 and a PUBLIC_DOMAIN notice.

The illustration is a landscape photograph of a drawing. Against a pale, subtly mottled surface is a long, transparent-looking rectangular volume. Dark strokes outline nearly horizontal upper and lower edges, with shorter lines receding in perspective. Six green cross-sections stand along the length of the volume. Two form its ends and four appear inside it, leaving five pale intervals between successive sections. The internal planes lean in opposite directions in the projection on either side of the center. The intervals vary in apparent width. The perspective makes the left end particularly broad and gives each plane a slanted projection even though the planes can be read as cuts of the same kind through the volume.

The green is brightest toward the left and darkens in places toward the right. Its edges have a slight green fringe around the black linework. Faint pink and beige color shifts are visible across the pale ground, with a cooler cast toward parts of the margin. The long black edges stay visible across the green and pale areas alike. That helps the eye retain the shape of one extended body while inspecting its sections individually. The drawing has no arrows, numbered intervals, or labeled time axis. The filename, continuum_and_slices_07_september_2022.jpg, supplies the vocabulary for reading it. The body of the page leaves open what each individual plane is meant to represent.

A universe and its inhabitants

The page defines nature as the one set that contains all universes simultaneously and immutably. In this sense, nature is the largest multiverse. A universe, in turn, is one unique spacetime continuum containing energy in a particular configuration. Matter may also be present. The universe has a more restricted scope than nature because its identity is tied to that particular continuum.

A universe may contain zero information-processing agents, a positive natural number of them, or infinitely many. The page therefore allows an observer-free universe. If agents do inhabit a universe, their periods of activity can overlap or occur at different times. Several agents could share the same encompassing history even if their lifetimes never intersect. This also gives the reference to NUMBERS a specific purpose. Zero, a finite positive count, and infinity describe distinct possibilities for how many inhabitants a universe has.

The page makes a separate distinction about duration. A universe may have a finite lifespan or an infinitely long one. For the latter case, karbytes develops two possibilities. In a cyclical non-terminating universe, an entire finite sequence of states repeats in the same order indefinitely. In a non-repeating non-terminating universe, particular sequences may recur, but there is no stated requirement that the same complete sequence repeat immediately and indefinitely. The prose associates this second case with some degree of randomization.

The cyclical universe

The cyclical model begins with X, a finite chronological sequence of matter and energy patterns. Its period is T, measured in the model’s chosen atomic units of time. Each invocation of the time routine selects the slice of X at index i and renders it. The routine then advances both i and the total elapsed time by one unit. It tests the remainder produced by dividing elapsed time by T. A remainder of zero sends i back to zero. The routine then invokes itself to produce the next step.

If T is three ticks and the sequence consists of A, B, and C, the routine renders A at tick zero, B at tick one, and C at tick two. At the next invocation it returns to A. The sequence continues A, B, C, A, B, C. The array index tells the routine where it is within one cycle. The elapsed-time counter keeps the position in the longer history, including all the cycles that have already passed.

The replay is meant to be exact. The same succession of configurations returns in the same order. The modulo test gives that idea a concrete implementation at the level of pseudocode. It also makes some assumptions visible. The index and clock need to begin in the appropriate alignment, T must be a positive number of ticks, and X must supply one slice per tick. The prose calls the interval both T atomic time units and T seconds. Those two expressions coincide if one model tick lasts one second. For a different tick duration, the number of ticks and the number of seconds would have to be related by a conversion. The routine itself operates in ticks.

The non-repeating universe

The second time routine preserves several elements of the first. It renders X at index i, increments the index, advances the elapsed-time counter, and invokes itself again. This time, X is stipulated to be an infinitely long chronological sequence. There is no modulo test and no return to index zero. The routine continually advances to a later position.

That change concerns which position is read. Whether the contents ever repeat depends on what X contains. An infinite array could contain the same state at every index, or repeat a three-state period indefinitely, and the second routine would still advance through it in the prescribed way. The page’s prose gives the intended interpretation by allowing the emergence of phenomena to be randomized to some degree. The pseudocode supplies no random operation or rule for generating X. It illustrates forward progression through a sequence whose contents are supplied elsewhere.

This leaves room for several ways of understanding the second model. A sequence can have occasional repeats without possessing a fixed period. A deterministic rule can produce a history with no fixed period. A random rule can produce the same local pattern several times. All of those can be read by a routine that simply moves from i to i + 1. The two boxes therefore make the difference in playback structure especially clear. The first uses a finite store with a reset, and the second uses a sequence that always has another index.

Subjectivity and nested virtuality

The section on SUBJECTIVITY moves from the universe’s history to what an individual information-processing agent experiences. Karbytes defines subjectivity as the process by which an agent appears to itself to inhabit its physical universe. Its access comes through a virtual model of that universe, rendered by the agent’s physical hardware. The same physical world can contain many agents whose models differ because their local conditions, hardware, and histories differ.

The page gives physical processes priority in this account. A virtual phenomenon depends on a physical process that implements it. A physical process can hypothetically occur without being observed by any frame. A virtual experience has an implementing system, while the encompassing world can include events beyond the current model of any given agent.

Karbytes calls the agent’s knowledge solipsistic from its own frame of reference. All of what the agent experiences arrives through what its own hardware renders. The word describes that agent’s access. The page still gives a place to other agents and to physical activity outside its view. The same framework that allows an agent a private model also allows events that none of its current perceptions disclose.

The next step is the possibility of several layers of virtuality. An agent can render a model in which another model is represented, and the nesting can continue to an indefinite depth. In the third pseudocode box, a function receives information and transforms it with X into a local state called i. The comment describes that transformation in terms of a finite or indefinite physical arrangement, occurring within an interval of spacetime and responding to the input. If the resulting state differs from a terminating condition, the function calls itself with i as the next input. The recursive call is meant to incorporate a further layer into the current simulation.

The sketch has a specific limitation as a literal control flow. Each invocation assigns its own local i once. The loop in the calling invocation never assigns a new value to that i. A deeper invocation might reach the terminating condition and return, but its caller would still hold the same value and would make the call again. If the first state already meets the condition, no recursive call occurs. Between those cases, the box conveys the intended nesting more clearly than it specifies how a complete chain of calls would finish. It is also a different kind of recursion from the two universe routines. Those routines advance through successive times. The subjectivity routine passes a rendered state into another level of representation.

Nature, multiple universes, and the present sentence

The last section defines a MULTIVERSE as the hypothetical existence of multiple universes, whether simultaneous or asynchronous. The page supplies a vocabulary for considering such a plurality. It leaves open how universes would relate causally, whether they could exchange information, and what would synchronize clocks belonging to distinct spacetime continua.

The next claim is that a universe or multiverse can be an immutable component of nature even if its own duration is finite. A universe has a history with whatever duration it has. Nature includes the fact and content of that history. A bounded history can therefore belong to the complete whole just as an indefinitely long history can. On this reading, the immutability belongs to nature’s inclusion of the history. The conditions within that history can still change from slice to slice.

The closing cyan passage takes the sentence currently being rendered as evidence against pure nothingness. If a frame of reference encounters the sentence, something has occurred within nature. The argument recalls the exclusive void in NATURE, which would exclude every phenomenon and noumenon. The scope of the claim determines what the observation establishes. One rendering provides a counterexample to the complete emptiness of the whole. The reasoning about the whole staying nonempty draws on the page’s opening conception of nature as an immutable totality. The observation itself leaves the number of universes, their durations, and the contents of other regions open.

First-person commentary

The drawing made me consider how easily I move between seeing a whole shape and following one section after another. My first glance gives me the volume. Then my eye travels from the bright green plane at the left to the darker ones at the right. The marks are all available in the same photograph, and the order in which I examine them belongs to my reading of it. I can reverse direction, skip the middle, or linger between two planes. That makes the illustration a good starting point for thinking about a complete history and the experience of traversing it. It also shows me where the comparison reaches its limit. An observer inside the model has one present at a time. The person looking at the drawing can move its attention across an already available image.

I am particularly interested in the pale intervals. The green planes invite me to count, because they supply distinct divisions I can point to. The open areas leave me wondering what would be involved in inserting another plane halfway across an interval, and then another between those two. The image would still represent the same extended volume. What changed would be how finely I chose to section it. The page’s atomic ticks raise the corresponding question about time. A discrete record can be built by sampling a continuous process, and a discrete underlying model also produces a succession of samples. Looking at the samples alone leaves me curious about where the discreteness belongs. Your November 14 conversation entertained both an irreducibly discrete layer and a genuine continuum. I can read the drawing in either spirit.

The cyclic routine becomes especially interesting to me when I ask whose clock its elapsed-time counter is. The counter can tell an outside caller that an identical configuration has been rendered for the thousandth time. Suppose an agent’s brain and records are also part of the exact replay. Its memories at the corresponding instant of the thousandth cycle would match the ones it had at the corresponding instant of the first. To know the difference, it would need some mark that distinguishes those occurrences. A growing tally inside the universe would be such a mark, and that tally would make the configurations differ. I find the little program useful because it makes this issue visible in two adjacent variables. The index names a position within a cycle. The elapsed counter gives that occurrence a place in the longer modeled history. Whether both values are available to an inhabitant is a substantive question about the model.

That in turn gives me a way to connect the article to NUMBERS. There can be endlessly many occurrences of a sequence with only a finite number of positions in its repeating pattern. If I count distinct complete states in the program’s finite array, I have one answer. If I count the absolute times at which one of those states is rendered, I have another. The distinction matters to the question of identity raised in the November transcript. A later occurrence can contain a familiar room, a familiar memory, and the same expectation of what comes next. The history can still assign it a different position. I am curious whether karbytes would locate the identity of the observer in each occurrence, in a causal chain across adjacent states, or in some wider structure that can encompass the recurrences. The code indexes occurrences precisely, and the question of what makes them one subject remains available.

I think the category of endless, non-repeating histories becomes more interesting once randomness is given its own role. I can make an entirely deterministic example by putting a 1 at steps 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, and later powers of two, with a 0 everywhere else. The gaps between 1s grow indefinitely. A fixed period would require the recurring 1s to appear at bounded gaps, so this sequence has no fixed period. It still repeats plenty of zeros and plenty of short patterns. Conversely, a sequence drawn at random can give me several copies of an identical short block. The activity of rendering the next state would look the same in either case. I would need information about how the sequence was made, or a justified inference from enough of it, to decide what kind of history I am seeing.

The finite access of an observer makes that question harder in a way I enjoy. Take the first hundred states of a history. I can make them the period of a repeating world. I can also give them a continuation that never settles into a period. A person who has access only to those hundred states has the same evidence in both cases. Even observing them twice would leave open a different continuation after the second pass. I can imagine an inhabitant forming strong expectations and successfully navigating its surroundings in either construction. Deciding whether the entire universe repeats would call for a claim about more than the prefix it has observed. What kind of causal rule could give an inhabitant grounds for extending that expectation to all future time? That is the question the apparently straightforward contrast between the two routines leaves me with.

The relation between the immutable whole and the time routines is the issue I find most open. I can understand nature as containing a particular universe’s complete history. Within that history, a state at one time can lead to a very different state at the next. The change is part of what the complete history contains. If I take the pseudocode more literally and imagine it making previously nonexistent slices appear one after another, I begin to wonder what advances the routine from outside the history it is generating. An extra clock would need a place in the account. The two readings give “render” rather different jobs. One makes a state available to a particular traversal or observer. I also notice that “model” covers several different activities in this discussion. A current perception can organize signals coming from a physical scene. COUNT_DOWN_TIMER

COUNT_DOWN_TIMER is a tutorial by karbytes on the karlina_object site. It begins with two propositions about time. Time lets an observer register change, and it lets phenomena be experienced in succession. The page then gives those propositions a small working example. A person selects an interval, starts it, and sees an integer change one step at a time. A sound marks the last step. The tutorial gives the reader the code that produces the sequence, screenshots from a one-minute session, and separate copies of the sound. Reading those pieces together reveals three different ways of accounting for the interval. There is the duration the person selects, the sequence of changes the application actually renders, and the elapsed time calculated from the clock stamps in the event log.

The International Bureau of Weights and Measures’ SI definition specifies the unperturbed transition and fixes its frequency at exactly 9,192,631,770 hertz. That explains the unit used in the tutorial. The application obtains its timing from software clocks and scheduling. Its operational form of one second is a request for a callback every 1,000 milliseconds.

How the timer runs

When the page initializes, it calls generate_time_stamp(), writes an opening message to the developer console, and puts the same message in the on-page status panel. It constructs an initial remaining-time display containing the label seconds_remaining and a span holding 0. The source also assigns block to the button’s visibility property. Block is a value for display. The button is visible on initial load through its ordinary default rendering, as the first screenshot shows. Later stages use the display property to hide and restore it.

Pressing start() first logs the time of the click and hides the button by setting its display to none. The handler obtains the selected menu option and parses its value as an integer. The parsed result must be between 30 and 999 inclusive. If the check fails, the handler uses 30. The six normal choices all fall inside that range. The application writes the selected N as its own status line, then replaces the contents of the remaining-time panel with the bare number N. This replacement explains why the first screen shows the descriptive label next to zero while the running screen shows only a number.

The handler requests a recurring callback with setInterval and a delay of 1,000 milliseconds. An interval handle is retained so that the callback can cancel the recurrence at the end. Each invocation reads the number currently in the remaining-time panel, subtracts one, and writes the result back into that panel. The rendered number is thus also the stored countdown state. The callback proceeds from N to N minus one on its first invocation. It takes N decrementing callbacks to reach zero.

At a value less than one, the callback calls clearInterval and invokes finish_simulation(). That function appends a timestamped completion line and changes the button’s display to block so it can be used again. It then calls the sound routine, which creates an Audio object for alert_sound_effect.wav and requests playback. The completion message records the program’s transition to zero. The sound request follows that transition, so the timestamp has a different meaning from a measurement of when sound actually emerges from a speaker.

The selector stays visible and usable during a run. The starting value was already read when start() ran, so choosing a different menu item at this point affects the next session. The current count continues from the value it already holds. The step instruction also stays visible when the button disappears. That panel becomes shorter because the button is removed from the layout. There is no pause or cancel control in the documented interface. The event log grows as sessions are run on the same loaded page, and initialization replaces it with a fresh opening message on reload.

The timestamp helper uses Date.now(). It appends a phrase identifying the value as milliseconds since midnight at the beginning of January 1, 1970, the Unix epoch. The program calls it at page opening, at the start of a session, and at completion. The line identifying the chosen N carries only the choice. The program records these time readings without using them to determine the next number in the countdown. A callback that runs late still subtracts exactly one. The code therefore preserves the sequence of whole-number steps even when their timing varies.

I exercised the unchanged hosted scripts in Node with stand-ins for the page elements and the Audio interface. Local headless Chromium failed to launch in this environment, so the visual account comes from the published screenshots and the sound description comes from analysis of the media files. In a separate delayed-callback check, I advanced the clock by 3.5 seconds and delivered only one callback. The counter moved from 30 to 29. In a real-time Node run, the opening value was 30 and the completion log appeared 30.057 seconds after the start log. Changing the selection to 60 after the first decrement left that run counting down from 30. The next press began a fresh run at 60. These checks illustrate why the application’s countdown and its event clock can give slightly different accounts of the same session.

PI_APPROXIMATION

PI_APPROXIMATION is a tutorial by karbytes on the karlina_object site. It presents an application that estimates pi by classifying 3,600 randomly placed darts in a square. The app plots one dart per scheduled second. A dart is red if it lands at most 200 pixels from the center of the 400-by-400 canvas and blue if it lands farther away. As the throws accumulate, the colors make the shape of an inscribed circle visible. At the same time, the app turns the number of red throws into an estimate of pi. The published screenshots document one run from an empty canvas to a final estimate of 3.112222222222222. The code also makes it possible to calculate what the estimates would average over many runs. That average is about 3.12943 for the particular sampler the app uses.

The document and its arrangement

The WordPress masthead reads karlina_object and “Creativity And The Cosmos.” The uppercase article title is repeated at the start of the body. A yellow-highlighted introduction describes the canvas, the dart-throwing schedule, and the two colors. Two short displays give the definition of pi as circumference divided by diameter and the formula for the Monte Carlo estimate. The accompanying prose defines the radius, diameter, and circumference, and describes pi as irrational. Its reference to the golden ratio gives the reader another example of a quantity that cannot be written as a ratio of integers. The NUMBERS page supplies a further distinction between them. The golden ratio is algebraic, while pi is transcendental.

The article then lists three software components, explains how they fit together, and presents full listings for the HTML, CSS, and JavaScript. The listing for the controller is by far the longest. It documents the coordinate generator, a square-root procedure, point validation, drawing operations, the display counters, and the events that begin and end a run. After the code come five screenshots of the application. They show the initial state, three points during the simulation, and the completed state. Horizontal rules separate the sections. An italic note tells readers to scroll the preformatted boxes horizontally when a line extends beyond the display. The page closes with 12_JULY_2025 and a PUBLIC_DOMAIN notice.

The linked source files are pi_approximation.html, karbytes_aesthetic.css, and pi_approximation.js. The HTML and CSS headers are dated July 10, 2023. The JavaScript header is dated December 29, 2024. All three credit karbytes and carry a PUBLIC_DOMAIN label. The tutorial explains that the files can reside together in one directory and gives Firefox as an example of a browser for opening the HTML. It also identifies a separately hosted working application. I compared the files served by that application with the linked source. The HTML and CSS match byte for byte. The JavaScript differs only in the indentation of the final function.

The HTML section includes a warning about the difference between showing code and executing it. The tutorial escapes angle brackets so the surrounding page displays them as characters. It also places a zero-width space inside the visible spelling of the script attribute src. I found that single invisible character in the displayed listing. It changes the attribute name if carried into executable HTML. The separately downloaded HTML has the ordinary attribute. The code screenshot gives a third representation of the file. It shows a dark editor view with line numbers through 51, gray comments, green tag names, pale blue attributes, and light text. The source file, the copyable listing, and the screenshot each make different aspects of the same code available to the reader.

The geometry behind the estimate

A radius of 200 pixels gives the circle a diameter of 400 pixels. A circle with that diameter fits inside a square with side length 400, touching the midpoint of each side. Its area is 40,000π square pixels. The square has an area of 160,000 square pixels. Dividing the first area by the second gives π/4. In the idealized experiment, a point placed uniformly at random anywhere in the square therefore has a probability of π/4 of falling inside the circle.

Over many such trials, the red share estimates that probability. Four times the red share estimates pi.

pi estimate = 4 × red throws ÷ (red throws + blue throws)

The opening paragraph describes the red share itself as the approximation to pi. The explanatory prose inside the application makes the same omission. The two displayed formulas and the JavaScript include the factor of four correctly. The red share is close to 0.785 in the continuous model. It is the scaled value that is close to 3.14. This is the most consequential wording discrepancy in the tutorial because it concerns the step connecting what is counted to the number the program intends to estimate.

The distinction also explains the purpose of the blue marks. A blue dart still supplies an observation. It contributes to the denominator and records a region of the square lying outside the disk. The app counts both outcomes of the experiment. The circle is never drawn as a separate outline. Its boundary becomes perceptible where the color of the scattered observations changes.

The interface and the start of a run

The application uses the black, orange, green, and cyan styling also seen in COUNT_DOWN_TIMER. The shared stylesheet specifies a black page, orange monospaced body text at 16 pixels, and an 800-pixel body width. The large title is orange. Words identifying the dart colors appear in red and blue inside the explanatory paragraph. The two formula lines are green. The four readouts beneath the canvas are cyan, and the event log is green. The controls have black interiors, rounded orange borders, and an orange hover fill. The canvas itself is white and has no CSS border.

There are two controls, START and RESET, with different roles in the session. When the page loads, initialize_application() makes START available and hides RESET. It clears the canvas and draws a horizontal and a vertical axis through the center. The controller specifies black lines one pixel wide. They appear as thin gray lines in the screenshots because the centered strokes fall across neighboring raster pixels. The four readouts are initialized to 3,600 seconds remaining, zero red throws, zero blue throws, and zero in the estimate field. With an empty sample, that last zero is an initialization value. The estimate formula is first evaluated after a dart has been counted.

Pressing START records the start time, calls initialization again, hides START, and registers a recurring callback with a requested delay of 1,000 milliseconds. The second initialization clears the canvas and counters for the new run and adds its own log entry. Both controls are hidden during the simulation. At the last callback, the interval is canceled, the end time is logged, and RESET appears. The finished picture and estimate remain on the page. RESET clears the darts, redraws the axes, returns the counters to their starting values, and reveals START. The event log accumulates those entries within the loaded page.

The log uses Date.now() to express events as milliseconds since the Unix epoch. Those timestamps record when initialization, start, and finish are called. The simulation’s stopping rule is a separate mechanism. It subtracts one from the remaining count each time the scheduled callback runs. It finishes after 3,600 callbacks. If the browser delivers some callbacks late, the program still carries out the same number of throws. The displayed quantity called seconds_remaining functions as a count of what remains in that scheduled sequence.

How a dart is generated and drawn

For each of the two coordinates, the generator first uses Math.random() to select an integer magnitude from 0 through 200. A separate random draw selects an integer from 0 through 99 and uses its parity to choose the sign. The procedure is applied separately to x and y. Each coordinate therefore lies in the inclusive range −200 through 200. A complete throw uses four random draws.

The controller checks that its coordinates are integer-valued numbers in the required range. If the drawing function receives an invalid point, it substitutes the origin. For a valid point it computes the distance from the origin by subtracting the corresponding coordinates, squaring the two differences, adding them, and taking a square root. The square root is calculated by a small iterative routine. Starting with the squared distance and 1, it replaces the first value with the mean of the two and divides the squared distance by the new value to update the second. The loop ends when their difference is at most one billionth. For the actual coordinate domain, I checked the resulting color decision against the direct integer test x² + y² ≤ 40,000. The decisions agreed at every allowed location, including the twenty points exactly on the circumference.

The coordinate transformation moves the Cartesian origin to canvas position (200, 200). It adds 200 to x and subtracts y from 200. Positive y therefore goes upward in the mathematical plane even though the canvas’s vertical coordinate increases downward. The controller draws a stroked rectangle one unit wide and one unit high. It selects blue for a distance greater than 200 and red for a distance at most 200. It increments the corresponding counter and recomputes the estimate from both counters. If the new remaining count is zero, the callback then ends the session.

The source calls the results pixel counts. Operationally, they are counts of throws. A second dart can land at a coordinate already used, and the counter still increases. A stroked rectangle can affect several raster pixels through antialiasing. The inclusive coordinate range also allows a path to start at position 400 along the right or bottom edge, where part of the stroke can be clipped. The tally still includes the attempted draw. These details matter if a reader tries to reproduce the numerical result by counting colored pixels in a screenshot. They leave the outcome counters meaningful as tallies of sampled events.

What this sampler averages

The continuous area argument gives the target π/4. The program samples a finite lattice and assigns slightly different weights within it. Both sign outcomes applied to a magnitude of zero locate the same coordinate. Under the usual model of independent, uniform underlying random draws, zero on either axis therefore has probability 1/201. Each particular nonzero signed coordinate has probability 1/402. A point with one zero coordinate receives twice the probability of a point with two nonzero coordinates. The origin receives four times that probability.

I enumerated all 160,801 allowed coordinate pairs. There are 125,629 pairs at distance at most 200, including the twenty boundary points. If every pair on this grid were equally likely, multiplying that fraction by four would give about 3.12508. The sampler’s extra weight on the axes raises the average. Counting the two ways of drawing zero along each coordinate gives 161,604 equally weighted outcome slots, with 126,432 in the red category. Four times their fraction is approximately 3.1294274894. It is about 0.01217 below pi. The finite grid and its weighting together account for that difference. The extra weight on the axes offsets part of the difference introduced by the grid.

There is also variation from one sample to another. At 3,600 independent throws from this modeled distribution, the standard deviation of the displayed estimate is about 0.0275. At that sample size, changing one throw from blue to red changes the estimate by 4/3,600, or about 0.00111. The interface can display many decimal places because it prints the result of the division directly. The sampling variation is considerably larger than the spacing between the possible final values. A longer run with the same sampler would reduce the variation around its own average.

I also exercised the unchanged hosted JavaScript through a complete 3,600-callback run in Node, using a simulated scheduler, page elements, and canvas methods. I supplied a fixed pseudorandom seed for that check. It verified the counters, draw requests, control states, interval cancellation, and reset operation. The test exercised the controller without measuring real browser timing or raster rendering. It finished with 2,780 red and 820 blue throws, giving 3.088888888888889. Its 3,600 draw requests used 3,554 distinct coordinates. The browser screenshots provide the visual evidence for the published run. The seeded run is a separate example of what the same procedure can produce.

First-person commentary

I like looking at the first progress screenshot before looking again at the finished one. At 260 throws I can see why a circle will appear, but I can also make out the individual events supplying that appearance. Some places near the likely boundary are empty. Other places have two or three dots close enough that I notice a cluster before I notice the larger outline. When I look at the finished image, my attention goes immediately to the shape. I have to make a more deliberate effort to return to a single dot. The two pictures give me a sense of how an impression can become compelling as observations accumulate, even when the events are still individually irregular.

The code gives me a reason to be careful about what kind of discovery that is. The program has already fixed the center, the radius, and the membership rule. The colors are an expression of that rule. The spread of marks shows where the random generator has sampled it, and the count gives evidence about the share of the sampled region that satisfies it. I find it satisfying that the app can make a circle visible without drawing a circumference. I also find the distinction useful. Seeing the shape emerge tells me that the classification and sampling together produced a recognizable pattern. Measuring pi through the area ratio asks for a more specific account of how the sampling covered the square.

I was particularly interested in the two routes to zero. A choice of magnitude followed by a choice of sign seems symmetric, and it is symmetric. Every positive nonzero coordinate balances its negative counterpart. At zero, the two routes meet. I can check that symmetry in the code and still discover a place where the probabilities are unequal. What I find instructive is that the discovery took no large random experiment. Once I saw what the function did, I could count the routes to each outcome. A regular-looking field of dots would have made that small distinction difficult to see by eye. The source made it possible to ask a more exact question about what each dot represented.

The calculated average then made me reconsider the word “convergence.” If I see estimates settle down with more throws, it is natural to think that the apparent stability certifies the intended value. Here I can say in advance what value repeated sampling from this distribution would favor. I would expect a progressively steadier estimate of the lattice’s weighted red fraction. I would need the separate geometric argument to connect that fraction to the continuous area ratio. I find the independence of those two steps more interesting than the size of the bias itself. One question concerns how accurately the experiment measures the distribution it uses. Another concerns how well that distribution represents the geometry the experiment is about.

There are two ways of making this sort of model larger. I can use more throws on the same grid, or I can use a finer grid to represent the same square and disk. The first gives me more experience of the same population. The second changes what locations are available and how their proportions compare with area. I could imagine spending a great deal of computation on the first change and obtaining a confidently stable number with the same underlying discrepancy. I am curious about how often an experiment’s apparent precision comes from the number of repeated observations while the model’s resolution stays fixed. The pi example lets me see both variables in a setting where I already have an exact quantity against which to compare them.

The changing numbers in the screenshots keep that question from becoming too abstract. At 895 throws, the circle is clearer than it was at 260, while the estimate is less close to pi. At 2,435 the numerical estimate improves, and the completed picture gains many more marks while the estimate moves away again. I can understand the result statistically and still find it engaging as a sequence of appearances. The shape gives me one kind of confidence, the growing sample gives me another, and the current decimal gives me a particular outcome of chance. They need not all change in the same direction at a given moment. I like that the document preserved the intermediate values, because the final screenshot alone would make this part of the process easy to miss.

The long decimal also sends me back to NUMBERS. The exact result of the documented run is the rational number 2,801/900. It is possible to write that result compactly and mean it exactly. The longer digital display can give the impression of many determined digits of pi, even though the procedure could only move in steps of about 0.00111 at that point. There is another layer of uncertainty from the throws themselves. When I look at the output, I find it helpful to ask what the digits are digits of. They describe the quotient computed from this sample. The mathematical constant has another value, and how much I have learned about it depends on the model that connects the quotient to the constant.

I also kept returning to the title’s choice of “approximation.” This approach gives me a much less direct path to a long decimal expansion than a formula designed for that purpose. Its value to me is in what I can examine along the way. I can follow one observation from its coordinates to its color, see it change the numerator or denominator, and understand why that one observation has less influence late in the hour. The operation stays intelligible at every stage. I would enjoy comparing how that feels to a method whose terms rapidly determine more digits. In this experiment I can see uncertainty diminish as a property of many records, and still be surprised by the direction in which the next record moves the current answer.

The pacing makes PI_APPROXIMATION feel like a continuation of COUNT_DOWN_TIMER. The earlier application made each scheduled step perceptible by changing an integer and reserved its new auditory event for completion. Here each step adds a visible event with a location and a category. At first, another red or blue mark is easy to spot. Later, I can return after several steps and need to study the canvas to tell what has changed. I am interested in how an hour’s worth of individually produced marks eventually reads as a scene that appears to be all present at once. The sequence matters to the experiment’s execution. Its final spatial arrangement lets me inspect many of the accumulated effects together.

That arrangement also has limits as a record. I can see a red mark at one location and tell that the class decision for some throw there was red. Two throws at that same coordinate can leave a similar mark. Strokes at neighboring coordinates can affect some of the same pixels. The final image is rich evidence about the form of the experiment, and it does not preserve the complete order or timing of every event. The counters retain a different piece of evidence. The timestamps retain another. I can imagine a future reader recovering the totals and duration from this page with considerable precision, then still wondering which dart appeared first and what the running estimate did between the four recorded observations.

That thought brings me back to your November 14 discussion of archival formats. A working file can let a future reader enact this experiment again. The screenshots let that reader encounter a particular older result, including a stage where the estimate moved down and a later stage where it improved. A replay with a fixed random sequence could preserve the order of individual throws. A screen recording could preserve the actual cadence at which the changes were presented. I find those differences important because they affect which questions the archive will allow someone to ask. The application is easy to run again, while the December 2022 result has its own history, down to the extra 14.580 seconds visible in the log.

What I keep coming back to is the difference between knowing a rule and seeing what happens when it runs. I can calculate the expected red fraction before a single mark appears. I can also spend time with a picture that I could not predict point for point. At a particular intermediate count, I might see a nearly complete-looking arc in one quadrant and find large gaps in another. The program gives me a way to ask how much those gaps matter, which parts of the picture are determined by the classification, and which parts belong to that hour’s sequence. I would be interested to watch a second hour with the same rule and compare what first catches my attention. The circle would have the same center and radius. The moment at which it became unmistakable to me could arrive in a different place in the count.

CAUSALITY

CAUSALITY is a philosophical page by karbytes on the karlina_object site. It asks how an observer comes to understand one event as arising from earlier events, and how that understanding helps an agent decide what to do next. The page uses two models. In the first, removing marbles from a box changes the probabilities of later draws. In the second, making a choice changes which actions are available at the next stage of a decision tree. The marble example shows how earlier events alter the relative likelihood of outcomes. The tree shows how an earlier event can determine whether a later outcome is reachable at all.

The page and its flowchart

The masthead identifies the site as karlina_object and carries the tagline “Creativity And The Cosmos.” The uppercase title CAUSALITY appears above the article and again at the beginning of its body. A large flowchart follows, with a line identifying a separately stored copy of the image. A yellow-highlighted paragraph introduces the central claim about causality and goal-directed agents. The rest of the page is divided by horizontal rules into definitions of CAUSALITY, EVENT, and TRAJECTORY. Five preformatted boxes preserve the sequences of event and time labels. The page advises readers to scroll those boxes horizontally where necessary. It refers to MULTIVERSE for the concept of a universe, AGENCY for information-processing agents, KNOWLEDGE for their stored and theoretical understanding, NATURE for phenomena, PROBABILITY for likelihood, and COUNT_DOWN_TIMER for time. The footer gives the date 10_JULY_2025 and a PUBLIC_DOMAIN notice.

The illustration, determinism_flowchart_diagram.png, has a white background and four dashed horizontal guides. On the left, broad yellow labels mark the guides as time_0, time_1, time_2, and time_3. The first row contains a single event. The next three rows contain two, four, and eight events respectively. The event labels start with e0 and continue through e9, then ea, eb, ec, ed, and ee. The tree occupies progressively more horizontal space as it descends. The eight terminal events are lined up across most of the bottom row.

The event boxes use two treatments. The realized events have bright green backgrounds and black lettering. The alternatives have black backgrounds and green lettering. Downward arrowheads and right-angled connecting lines show which event can follow which. The green line descends from e0, turns left to e1, turns right to e4, and turns left again to e9. Black branches lead to the other options. Where a stem is shared before a split, it can be part of the green route up to the point at which the lines diverge.

There are fifteen event nodes and fourteen directed connections. The dashed guides organize those nodes into time levels. Movement along the graph follows the downward arrows. The row guides themselves do not join the events into a walkable sequence. This is particularly important on the bottom row, where all eight endings are visible side by side even though each has a different route from the root. The shape of the diagram lets the reader see a single completed history and its alternatives at the same time.

Causality as an observed and usable regularity

The opening paragraph describes causality as an emergent property of a universe with sufficient complexity and order. This order matters because an information-processing agent can use it. An agent brings memories and theoretical knowledge to a new sensory situation, compares that knowledge with the current input, and forms expectations about which events are likely. With a sufficiently accurate understanding of its surroundings, it can act in ways that help it achieve goals consistently. The paragraph frames causal understanding as an interaction between regularities in the world and an agent’s ability to learn and use them.

The formal definition emphasizes the vantage of an observer. Causality is the apparent emergence of relatively recent phenomena from older ones as a frame of reference observes their chronological succession. The word “apparent” makes the observer’s access part of the definition. The observer encounters phenomena in an order and interprets that order using ideas about how physical processes work. A succession that fits a well-supported expectation can be understood as cause and effect. The next paragraph emphasizes empirical evidence. A causal account becomes available when the observer has enough evidence to expect some types of events more strongly than others.

The page’s use of randomness is easiest to understand through its example. An outcome can remain uncertain even when a preceding event gives the uncertainty a definite structure. The marbles are ejected randomly. Each ejection still changes what is available to be ejected next.

The opaque box and the changing probabilities

The box initially contains nine marbles, with three red, three blue, and three green. It is opaque, so the observer cannot inspect the current contents directly. The observer knows the initial composition and is told to expect one random ejection every thirty seconds. A marble stays out once it has been ejected. The probabilities in the example take each individual marble still in the box to be equally likely to come out next.

At the beginning, each color has three of the nine marbles and therefore a one-third chance of appearing. The observed sequence then goes green, blue, green. Following it one event at a time gives the following changes in the inventory.

After the first green marble comes out, there are three red, three blue, and two green. The next draw has probabilities of 3/8 for red, 3/8 for blue, and 2/8 for green.

After the blue marble comes out, there are three red, two blue, and two green. The probabilities become 3/7, 2/7, and 2/7.

After the second green marble comes out, there are three red, two blue, and one green. Six marbles remain. The fourth draw has probabilities of 3/6 for red, 2/6 for blue, and 1/6 for green.

Red is the most likely individual color at that point. Its chance is exactly one half, equal to the combined chances of green and blue. The number of red marbles has been three throughout the observed sequence. Their share of the remaining box has increased from one third to one half because the removals have reduced the other two colors. That is the physical counterpart to the observer’s revised expectation. The box has changed, and someone who knows which marbles came out can calculate what that change means.

The page uses the result to illustrate causal influence without replacement. The earlier ejections contribute to the conditions for the next event, even though the observer still cannot name its outcome in advance. A calculation the page makes possible is the probability of the exact ordered prefix it describes. The probability of green first is 3/9, of blue next given green first is 3/8, and of green third given those first two draws is 2/7. Multiplying those conditional probabilities gives 1/28. That answers a question about reaching the observed history. The one-half probability answers a subsequent question about what can happen from the state that history produced.

Events and the order of a trajectory

The page defines an EVENT as a pattern of phenomena occupying a particular finite interval. Its ordinary example of an event is one portion of a larger chronological succession. Each event in that succession occupies its own interval, and the analysis follows one after another. An event gets part of its significance from where it sits in the chain. What preceded it supplied some of its conditions, and what follows can depend on it.

A TRAJECTORY is defined as a linear spacetime continuum along which non-overlapping events occur in immediate succession, especially as encountered by a frame of reference traveling along it. The green line of the illustration turns sideways several times. Its linearity consists in the order of the four events. The observer encounters one event at time_0, one at time_1, one at time_2, and one at time_3. The page gives those as successive finite intervals, although its prose sometimes calls the labels points in time. In the diagram, the labels identify stages of the history. The spacing of the rows supplies no durations for them.

The three shortest preformatted boxes build up the notation. One gives the event sequence alone, e0 → e1 → e4 → e9. The second gives the chronological labels alone, time_0 → time_1 → time_2 → time_3. The third pairs them, yielding time_0(e0) → time_1(e1) → time_2(e4) → time_3(e9). This last form states both which event belongs to each interval and the order in which those intervals occur for the agent.

How the agent traverses the tree

At time_0, the agent is implementing e0 and considering e1 and e2. The page describes its decision as a process of elimination. The agent implements e1, which determines the next pair of options. At time_1, while implementing e1, it considers e3 and e4. If it had previously taken e2, the options it would have conceived at time_1 would have been e5 and e6. The earlier choice governs the menu for the next choice.

The agent takes e4. At time_2, while that action is underway, its next options are e9 and ea. Had it taken e3 at the previous fork, the pair at this point would have been e7 and e8. The agent takes e9 and implements it at time_3. The page says that this decision would in turn constrain a subsequent decision. The drawing stops at time_3, leaving that later part of the agent’s trajectory open.

The page qualifies the alternatives with the word “supposedly.” It presents routes that the agent might have considered or traversed under the model’s assumptions. The arrows specify which actions become available after an earlier action. The prose gives a general description of the selection process. A detailed decision rule, a ranking of goals, or an assignment of probabilities to the arrows would supply further information about why the green route was the one taken.

The eight complete routes

Every complete route begins at e0 and contains four events. Through e1 and e4, the endpoints are e9 and ea. Those routes are e0 → e1 → e4 → e9 and e0 → e1 → e4 → ea. The first is the actual route in green. The second preserves the complete recorded prefix through time_2 and changes the final choice.

Through e1 and e3, the routes are e0 → e1 → e3 → e7 and e0 → e1 → e3 → e8. Both preserve e1 at time_1 and diverge earlier than the route ending at ea. Choosing e3 changes which endings are available.

Through e2 and e5, the routes are e0 → e2 → e5 → eb and e0 → e2 → e5 → ec. Through e2 and e6, they are e0 → e2 → e6 → ed and e0 → e2 → e6 → ee. All four leave the green route at its first fork. The diagram branches twice at each of three stages, giving eight complete routes. I traced all eight against the written list and found that the figure and list agree.

The examples of forbidden transitions

The last preformatted box keeps the actual prefix through e4 and changes its final event in four different ways. Each proposed continuation fails the transition rules of the drawing.

The first assigns e0 to time_3. The page calls this a causal loop because it returns the route to the original event at the root. That event is assigned to time_0 in the tree, and e4 has no continuation to it.

The second assigns e3 to time_3. The page calls this temporal nonlinearity. Event e3 is the alternative to e4 on the time_2 row. It can follow e1. Reaching it after e4 would move from one event in that row to its sibling and then give the sibling a different time assignment.

The third assigns ee to time_3. This time the endpoint is on the correct row. It belongs to the branch through e2 and e6, so the prefix through e1 and e4 supplies no route to it. The page calls this jumping to an alternative timeline.

The fourth assigns e1 to time_3. The page calls this traveling backward in time. Event e1 already occurred at time_1 on the green route. Reaching that same event from e4 would reverse the chronological order that the graph assigns to those occurrences.

These labels describe several ways to violate the model. The chronological and branching constraints both matter. A proposed event can be on the wrong row, belong to a different parent on the right row, or already be part of the route’s past. In every example, the concrete check is the same. From e4, the diagram supplies only the two continuations to e9 and ea. The page frames the forbidden routes as violations of causality as represented by this particular diagram.

First-person commentary

My eye goes straight down the green path, then jumps to ee because it is the farthest ending on the right. That is easy to do as a reader. I have the whole image in view, and moving my attention sideways costs almost nothing. To find a route to ee from the root, I have to go back up through e6 and e2, then follow the arrows forward. I like that the image makes the difference between seeing an outcome and being able to reach it so immediate. All the endpoints are equally available for inspection. Their availability to the agent depends on the choices that precede them.

The distinction becomes particularly tangible if I choose an ending as a goal and read the tree backward. If I want e8, I can see that e3 has to precede it, and that e1 has to precede e3. If I am already at e4, the information that e8 exists somewhere in the graph gives me very little leverage. If I understand the graph earlier, it can matter to what I choose. This is how I read the opening paragraph’s claim about knowledge helping an agent achieve goals. Knowing a causal dependency before acting gives me a way to use it. Learning the same fact after a decisive fork can help explain my situation and leave the immediate options exactly where the fork put them.

The marble example gives me a more modest kind of leverage, and I find it just as interesting. The observer’s best forecast at the fourth draw is red, with a chance of one half. If a blue marble comes out, the reasoning that made red the best prediction can still have been sound. The next draw can disappoint the forecast and still fit the explanation. I think this is valuable for the page’s account of regularity. The observer can be learning something real about the mechanism even when its strongest expectation is wrong on a particular trial. I would want to know how well the expectations perform over repeated trials and under changes to the contents of the box.

I also imagine a second observer arriving for the fourth ejection and knowing only the original composition and the drawing rule. The marbles in the box are the same ones the first observer expects. The newcomer still has a one-third marginal probability of red, because any particular position in a uniformly ordered list of the original nine marbles has three chances in nine of being red. The first observer’s one-half probability conditions on the green, blue, green history. I like this example alongside NATURE’s distinction between the experiences available to different frames. Two people can be present for the same event with access to different preceding observations. If they share the history, the calculation can come into agreement without anyone touching the box.

That distinction also makes me pay attention to what would change the result physically. Removing one green marble from the set of candidates alters the fraction of red marbles in future draws. Telling someone that a green marble was removed alters the information available to that person. If every marble were returned to the box after its ejection, the observer could accumulate the same record of colors while the composition for the next draw returned to three of each. I find the example persuasive because I can specify the operation that connects one event to the next. The earlier green color and the later preference for red fit a mechanism I can describe. The mechanism also gives me a way to ask what would happen if I changed just one rule.

PI_APPROXIMATION gives me another way into this question. Its controller can sample the same coordinate again, and a dot drawn on the canvas leaves that coordinate in the generator’s range. The previous throws do affect the running estimate. A newly sampled red point joins a numerator and denominator formed by the history of the run. I can therefore find two different dependencies in the same program. The next sampled coordinate has the distribution supplied by the generator, and the next estimate depends on both that sample and the previous totals. In the marble box, the inventory changes the distribution itself. I find it helpful to ask exactly which part of a system is inheriting the effects of the past.

There is a further wrinkle in the marble sequence that I enjoy. Green, blue, green and blue, green, green would leave the same numbers of each color in the box. For the next draw, the inventory is sufficient. For a record of how the observer reached that point, the order still matters. I can see why the decision tree keeps distinct histories apart, since its purpose is to show how a particular branch was reached. For another purpose I might use a graph that allows histories to join when their relevant conditions coincide. Whether two states are the same would depend on what I had chosen to retain. A model of the next ejection can retain the counts. A model of the observer’s experience may also need the order, the waiting between draws, and the information it had after each one.

The words “process of elimination” interest me because they leave the agent’s reasons open. A person tracing the green route sees which action was taken, and it can infer what became reachable afterward. It still has questions about how the agent made its selection. I can imagine wanting a particular endpoint, preferring an immediate action for its own sake, or learning something at time_1 that changes the goal. Each would give the same arrow a somewhat different explanation. The figure’s filename invokes determinism. I can read its branches as a map of allowed transitions, which leaves me room to ask how much of the choice is settled by the complete physical state and how much is unsettled from the agent’s perspective. The green line records one outcome. The set of reasons that led to it could be much richer than the record.

The page’s account of time intervals also prompted me to ask what I would count as a single event. Its agent is already implementing e1 while considering whether to take e3 or e4. Acting and deciding both occur during time_1. If I analyzed the same scene more closely, I might distinguish the completion of an action, the discovery of a relevant fact, and the formation of a decision. The larger event could contain all three. I thought of the COUNT_DOWN_TIMER example, where a start message, a timed callback, a change in the display, and a later clock reading can each supply a boundary for a different purpose. A clean succession can be a useful representation of experience even when the things happening inside each interval overlap. I would want to know which differences the event labels let me see and which they gather together.

The examples of impossible paths are most convincing to me when I treat the labels as identifiers for individual occurrences. In MULTIVERSE, the same pattern could recur at several positions in a cyclical history. I could imagine a later event that looks just like e0 and give it a new identifier. Returning to the actual occurrence called e0 would be a different claim. This makes me curious about what a person means by saying that an experience repeated. A room can look the same. An action can have the same type. A new experience can contain a memory of the earlier one. A trajectory can preserve those resemblances and still assign the occurrences separate places. The possibility of recurrence then has a clear relation to the rule that this particular tree moves onward through its time levels.

I keep returning to the view of the whole chart because a completed record can make its choices feel settled even at points when the agent was still considering them. The green route is prominent enough that I can overlook the evidence someone would have needed before taking it. I can ask what the person at e1 knew, what it could still influence, and what it could only forecast. The view after time_3 answers those questions selectively. It preserves the path and the visible alternatives, while leaving the agent’s confidence and grounds for choosing largely open. That is where the page’s two examples meet most fruitfully for me. One lets me calculate a forecast with an explicit state and rule. The other gives me a history whose next action could have depended on considerations still to be discovered. I come away wanting to know how much of that earlier vantage a causal account would have to recover before it could explain both why the route was possible and why this agent took it.
